### Monitoring of native forests in Argentina  

To ensure consistency of data, project activities developed at the local level will be monitored by the National Forest Monitoring System of Argentina (Argentina’s NFMS), which provides updated information on the location, area and condition of forests, either continuously or at regular intervals. The NFMS also serves for the monitoring of the implementation of the Forest Law plans and interventions.

The NFMS counts with the following components:  

#### Monitoring of Native Forests  
It provides information on the distribution and extent of forests and the annual changes it experiences, using remote sensing and GIS tools, and cartography. The main stages are the procurement and processing of satellite images, visual interpretation, and analysis by calculating forest areas, annual rate of forest loss, and CO₂ emission estimates, and lastly, the communication and dissemination of results, which is coordinated by the Forest Assessment System Management Unit (UMSEF).  

#### Deforestation Early Warning System  
This tool monitors the loss of native forest in an ongoing manner, through automated processes based on satellite images. The system, that is operational in the Parque Chaqueño Humid and Semi-arid sub-regions, and currently has been expanded to Arid and Serrano sub-regions, and to Argentine Yungas region, generates alerts every 16 days.  

#### National Native Forest Inventory  
Argentina has a First National Native Forest Inventory drawn up between 1998 and 2006 which entailed a first step towards understanding the extent and status of native forests. At present, the Second National Native Forest Inventory is being drawn up with the peculiarity that the plots will be permanently installed to allow re-measurement and monitoring of changes in the forest mass. The total number of plots that make up the network of plots suitable for re-measurement purposes is 4,158 distributed across the country. Throughout 2020, results will be available for all forest regions.  

#### Forest Statistics  
This is a DNB unit that compiles information on the sale of wood and its by-products, based on information provided by the provinces and other agencies, which leads to generating the Annual Forests Statistics report.  

### Other tools that will be used to support monitoring activities are:  

#### Forest Management, Control and Verification System “SACVeFor”  
Its fundamental objective is to manage the forest product traceability process by managing, controlling and verifying its different stages. It has a direct influence on the improvement and control of forest management and the use of its products, it increases the levels of formality in the sector and it increases transparency and social control over the use of the resource.  

#### Integrated Forest Information System (SIIF, in its Spanish acronym)  
This is a digital platform that aims at gathering information on native forests in a single site. This system contains a series of modules, among which is the module of the National Registry of Plans. This Registry shows information on the management and conservation plans, the formulation projects that originate them and on other interventions in native forests framed under the Forest Law. The projects implements within this proposal will be registered by this system.  

### The measurement in the field is carried out by DNB in its role of National Enforcement Authority in close collaboration with Local Enforcement Authorities and other local agencies.  
The information surveyed will be processed by Argentina’s NFMS and systematized by DNCC in its role of compiler and coordinator of the INGEI. Emissions reductions will be reported through the SNI-GEI-AR.  

### Project level monitoring and reporting  
Project-level monitoring will be undertaken in compliance with FAO policies. The project inception phase will include the formulation of a Theory of Change describing the causal relationship between outcomes that culminates with achieving the desired change and the underlying assumptions about how change will happen. FAO will ensure the existence of a well-designed, operational and effective impact monitoring and

### Inception Workshop  
The Inception Workshop is crucial to building ownership of the project results and to plan the first-year annual work plan. The Inception workshop will address several key issues including:  

- Assist all partners to fully understand and take ownership of the project.  
- Confirm the roles, support services and complementary responsibilities of FAO Country Office, Regional and Headquarter staff vis à vis the project team.  
- Discuss and confirm on the roles, functions and responsibilities within the project’s decision-making structures, including reporting and communication lines, and conflict resolution mechanisms.  
- Based on the project results framework, finalize the first annual work plan. Review and agree on the indicators, targets and their means of verification, and recheck assumptions and risks.  
- Provision of a detailed overview of reporting, monitoring and evaluation (M&E) requirements. The M&E work plan and budget will be agreed and scheduled.  
- Discussion of financial reporting procedures and obligations, and arrangements for annual audit.  
- Planning and scheduling of project Board meetings. Roles and responsibilities of all project organization structures will be clarified, and meetings planned. The first project Board meeting will be held within the first 12 months following the inception workshop.  
- An Inception Workshop Report will be a key reference document and will be prepared and shared with participants to formalize various agreements and plans decided during the meeting.  

### Annual Project Report  
This report will be prepared by the PMU, consolidated by the Project Manager, and finally approved by the Project Board to monitor progress made since project start and for the previous reporting period.  
The format and content of the annual report will be adjusted based on the simplified reporting regime which will be established for RBP by the GCF.  

### Mid-term Evaluation (MTE)  
The project will undergo an independent mid-term evaluation at the mid-point of project implementation. The mid-term evaluation will determine progress towards the achievement of outcomes and will suggest corrective actions if needed. It will focus on the effectiveness, efficiency and timeliness of project implementation; will highlight issues requiring decisions and actions; and will present initial lessons learned about project design, implementation and management.  

### Final Evaluation  
An independent Final Evaluation will be launched within six months prior to project’s actual completion date. It will aim at identifying project outcomes, their sustainability and actual or potential impacts, including inter alia global environmental benefits. It will also have the purpose of indicating future actions needed to assure continuity of the process developed through the project.  

Both mid-term and final evaluation will be managed by FAO OED and organized in coordination with the FAO Technical Advisor and the Project Board.  

During the final three months, the project team will prepare the Project Terminal Report. This comprehensive report will be made available to the public through the MAyDS web site. It will summarize the results achieved (objectives, outcomes, outputs), lessons learned, problems met and areas where results may not have been achieved. It will also lay out recommendations for any further steps that may need to be taken to ensure sustainability and replicability of the project’s results.

Any legislation related to ER rights. Until then – as indicated in the NDC (revised in 2016: Section 3.5, Use of Markets), “Any transfer of emission reduction units achieved in Argentina shall be explicitly authorized by the National Government and, unless otherwise provided for, all emission reductions in the country shall be counted towards achieving NDC goals” and reported to the UNFCCC.   

Nevertheless, the country’s legal framework provides the foundation to MAyDS’s mandate for receiving, managing and administering payments for forest environmental services, including GHG emission fixation. This is demonstrated by the implementation of previous payment for ecosystem services programmes related to the forest sector, and specific laws and resolutions as indicated below. In particular, according to the Argentinean legislation, MAyDS is in charge of protecting the value of forest environmental services throughout the territory, in synergy with the provinces within their jurisdictions, as established in article 12 of Law 26.331/2007. Therefore, the agreement reached in the context of the Federal Environment Council (COFEMA) between the provinces and MAyDS in relation to the GCF REDD+ RBPs excludes potential claims by the provinces, originally owners of natural resources, on the volume of ERs transacted with the GCF (COFEMA Resolution N. 424, 4 March 2020).   

Hereby follows a clear explanation in support to those assertions, with reference to the pertinent legal instruments:   

As Argentina is a federal state (by preamble of the Argentinean Constitution), provinces hold the original domain of the natural resources found in their territory, among them, native forests. “The provinces have the original dominion over the natural resources existing in their territory” (Section 124, par. 2 of the Constitution 76). “The authorities [provinces] shall provide for the protection of this right, the rational use of natural resources, the preservation of the natural and cultural heritage and of the biological diversity, and shall also provide for environmental information and education” (Article 41, par. 2).   

“The Nation shall regulate the minimum protection standards enforceable at national level while “the provinces those necessary to reinforce them, without altering their local jurisdictions” (Section 41 of the Constitution).   

In particular, as reflected in article 41 of the National Constitution, the law 25.675/2002 establishes minimum standards and conditions in order to guarantee common environmental protection measures to the entire territory. According to article 6, the Nation adopts the necessary conditions to guarantee the dynamics of ecological systems, maintain their carrying capacities and, in general, ensure environmental preservation and sustainable development. It also establishes in its Article 23 the Federal Environmental System implemented through COFEMA.   

In accordance with the constitutional provisions cited, the law on minimum standards for the environmental protection of native forests No26331/2007 (forest law 77) defines the minimum environmental standards to protect, enrich, restore, conserve, use and sustainably manage native forests. It also establishes a regime and criteria for the distribution of funds derived from environmental services (including GHG fixation) provided by native forests (Article 1).   

In particular, according to this law, the Nation has the competence to establish minimum standard rules for the entire national territory, in order to guarantee a minimum protection over forest resources, while each provincial jurisdiction can establish equivalent or higher standards.   

As it relates to ERs, article 5 of the forest law states that native forests provide the following environmental services, among others:  
a) conservation of biodiversity;  
b) soil and water quality conservation;  
c) greenhouse gas emissions fixation; and  
d) defense of cultural identity.  

MAyDS, designated as the competent national authority, is in charge to implement the national forest protection programme, aiming to “promote the creation and maintenance of sufficient and functional forest reserves for each eco-forest region of the national territory, in order to avoid adverse ecological effects and”

The law does not define Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) ownership rights. Instead, the law clearly states that MAyDS is responsible to protect the value of the environmental services provided by native forests (article 12, numeral c). Provinces concur in supporting MAyDS’ mandate. In this regard, the forest law includes principles and criteria for provincial jurisdictions to carry out land use planning of native forests, according to specified conservation criteria. Specifically, each jurisdiction must carry out land use planning of native forests in their territory according to criteria established in Annex I to the forest law.

“The National Enforcement Authority, i.e. MAyDS, at the request of the Enforcement Authority of each jurisdiction, shall provide necessary technical, economic and financial assistance to carry out land use planning of native forests in their jurisdiction (article 6). The national enforcement authority shall be the National Secretariat for the Environment and Sustainable Development 78 i.e. MAyDS (...) (article 11).”

Considering that Argentina is a federal state, it is therefore necessary to reach consensus and promote agreements between the provinces and the central administration, i.e. MAyDS, for the institutionalization of climate actions, projects and programmes throughout the national territory.

The Federal Environment Council (COFEMA)79 created by the General Law for the Environment (Annex I) constitutes the platform for decision-making and policy coordination among provincial jurisdictions and the central administration. It also instructs the National Executive Power to propose, as appropriate, the issuance of recommendations or resolutions to the COFEMA Assembly... for the adequate validity and effective application of laws of minimum budgets, the complementary role of provinces and their regulations in different jurisdictions... (Article 24 - Annex I). Among its objectives, COFEMA aims to formulate a comprehensive environmental policy and manages international financial resources derived from environmental projects (Articles 23 and 24 of the law, Annex I of the law, Article 2, subsection 1 and 11).

Since the enactment of the forest law and the approval of its regulatory decree, COFEMA’s resolutions and decisions have contributed to clarify and overcome undefined aspects or existing gaps related to the implementation of the forest law.

In relation to the subject matter, a resolution ratified by the Assembly of COFEMA confirms Argentina’s commitment (i) to participate at the GCF RBP Pilot Programme represented by MAyDS, as the national REDD+ entity, and (ii) for MAyDS to offer part of the achieved ER results during the period 2014-2016 to such scheme (resolution 424/2020, 4 March). The benefit-sharing distribution scheme proposed by MAyDS for this Funding Proposal was also presented and discussed and approved within COFEMA during that occasion.

Pursuant to the resolution 424/2020 signed by COFEMA, the country’s results-based payments proposal was declared “of federal interest” and all members, including therefore all the provincial jurisdictions, unanimously agreed that MAyDS was the entity designated to progress with the preparation of the GCF funding proposal and offer ER (as per section A of this funding proposal). The assembly also unanimously agreed to support the benefit-sharing distribution scheme presented by MAyDS, in relation to the funds that prioritize the Strategic Technical Guidelines of the Forest Law (approved by COFEMA Res. No 360/2018).

MAyDS, in its capacity as manager of the proceeds of the RBP proposal to GCF, shall prepare an annual report on the allocation of funds transferred during the previous year, indicating the amounts per province and forest category (Article 37, Forest Law). The report shall be discussed with COFEMA members.

The Law 27520 also established the National Climate Change Cabinet (GNCC in its Spanish acronym), technically coordinated by the MAyDS through its Secretariat of Climate Change and Sustainable Development. The GNCC plays a role of utmost importance as a platform for inter-ministerial and inter-sectoral climate change policy articulation at different levels with the participation of different bodies from the national and sub-national levels (provincial authorities). COFEMA is already ongoing, and it would be important to achieve further agreements on carbon rights topics.  

Argentina will include specific safeguards and criteria in the benefit sharing scheme to ensure that all parties, including vulnerable groups, have no complaints regarding the results that are being offered to GCF. These are:  
i) implementing a fair benefit-sharing mechanism;  
ii) ensuring fair compensation to the owners of plans in forest regions that have significantly contributed to achieving ERs;  
iii) paying special attention to the risks associated with double counting.  

Furthermore, to ensure a fair distribution of GCF-derived payments to vulnerable groups, MAyDS being entitled to administer ERs, “shall recognize and respect the rights of indigenous communities that have traditionally occupied the lands (article 19, forest law).”  

Lastly, it should be noted that in Argentina, following the guidelines of the UNFCCC, the construction of REDD+ pillars and implementation of related activities have been undertaken at a national or forest region level. This approach does not permit identifying stakeholders or specific actions that originated ERs in the territory (e.g., farm level) with scientific rigor and accuracy.  

To sum up:  
- The country will use REDD+ results attained across Argentine territory to meet its NDC within the context of UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement, including the ERs offered to the GCF RBP pilot programme, the volume of set aside/deactivated ERs as a mechanism to manage the risk of reversal, and those obtained from the project implementation. The NDC (revised in 2016: Section 3.5, Use of Markets) indicates that any reduction achieved is not transferable and shall not be used for other purposes except with the explicit authorization of the National Government (MAyDS);  
- According to Argentina legislation, the provinces originally own natural resources, including native forests, and forests environmental services (including GHG fixation) are administered by MAyDS, as reflected in the COFEMA Resolution 424/2020;  
- The provinces, through COFEMA, unanimously agreed that MAyDS was in charge to prepare the funding proposal package, for the GCF REDD+ pilot programme;  
- The provinces approved the benefit-sharing distribution scheme proposed by MAyDS, by COFEMA Resolution 424/2020;  
- According to Argentina legislation, and endorsed by COFEMA, MAyDS is authorized to regulate this subject in full respect of economic and environmental safeguards;  
- The GNCC (technically coordinated by the MAyDS) with the COFEMA are the most appropriate platforms for federal and multisectoral dialogues and articulations including for issues related to ER rights and related benefit-sharing arrangements and schemes.  

The reported rationale covenants that no competing claims will occur on the GCF’s payments received by Argentina through the GCF RBP pilot programme.  
A letter from MAyDS or another appropriate Argentinean authority is not necessary, as the analysis provided in this section contains relevant evidence that MAyDS is mandated by law to administer ERs titles.  

MAyDS’s key role in relation to issues concerning carbon credits is confirmed by the functions assigned to the entity by the climate change law (n.27520/2019). Particularly, MAyDS is designated as the national enforcement authority of this law, the UNFCCC, the Kyoto Protocol, the Paris Agreement and all the international conventions on climate change (article 6).  

Finally, the reference to the REDD+ national registry under development provides sufficient guarantees that no double counting of ERs or double payments will occur in Argentina.  

## Reference to registry of REDD+ projects  
MAyDS – as national enforcement authority of the Law 27520/2019 on Climate Change, and as focal point for the UNFCCC, the Kyoto Protocol, the Paris Agreement, and all other international conventions on climate change – set up a REDD+ registry where payments to be received from GCF will be recorded, aiming at ensuring transparency and avoiding double accounting and double use of the ERs that have already been paid.

# GCF/B.27/02/Add.02

### Secretariat’s assessment of FP142  

#### Proposal name: Argentina REDD-plus RBP for results period 2014-2016  
#### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)  
#### Country: Argentina  
#### Project/programme size: Medium  

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  

#### 1.1 Project background  

1. The project presents Argentina's REDD-plus results for the period 2014−2016, with an estimated reduction in emissions of 18.73 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) derived from reducing deforestation. These results have been reported to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) secretariat and published on the UNFCCC Lima REDD+ information hub following technical assessment. The results are presented to GCF for results-based payment (RBP) as part of the REDD-plus RBP pilot programme; they are fully compliant with the eligibility criteria set out in the terms of reference (TOR) for the pilot programme (decision B.18/07) and with UNFCCC standards. The results have undergone analysis based on the scorecard as shown in the annex.  

2. Argentina will use the proceeds from RBP to invest in additional activities that support the implementation of its nationally determined contribution (NDC) and in supporting the implementation of the National Action Plan on Forests and Climate Change (PANByCC). The RBP project will focus on the following components:  
   - Component 1: Territorial forest management;  
   - Component 2: Forest management with integrated livestock;  
   - Component 3: Forest restoration and enhanced response to forest fires; and  
   - Component 4: Technical capacities of national and provincial government institutions to address the drivers of deforestation and undertake forest monitoring and control.  

#### 1.2 Scorecard results  

3. As per decision B.18/07, the proposal has been assessed by the Secretariat with the support of land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) experts using the scorecard for REDD-plus RBP. The final score was discussed and agreed with the independent Technical Advisory Panel. The scorecard results are summarized in table 1 and presented in greater detail in the annex.  

##### Table 1: Scorecard results  

| Scorecard section     | Results                        |
|-----------------------|--------------------------------|
| Carbon elements       | Score: 41/48 All criteria “pass” |
| Cancun Safeguards     | All criteria “pass”            |

### 7. The Secretariat invites stakeholders and partners to recall that the objective of the request for proposal (the REDD-plus RBP pilot programme) is to gather experience to further improve the procedural and technical elements of REDD-plus RBP using GCF resources in a learning stage (TOR, p.2). As part of this learning stage, the Secretariat acknowledges that there is further room for improvement of the technical instruments in any future configuration of REDD-plus RBP under GCF, which is to be designed as reflected in the mid-term review presented for consideration at the twenty-fifth meeting of the Board.

## II. Assessment of performance against investment criteria  
### 2.1 Impact potential                               Scale: High  

### 9. The total volume of REDD-plus results achieved by Argentina and reported in the REDD-plus technical annex to its third biennial update report (BUR) for the 2014–2016 period was 165,172,705.42 tCO2eq (44,409,046 tCO2eq for 2014, 59,006,338 tCO2eq for 2015 and 61,757,321 tCO2eq for 2016). This is equivalent to an approximate 56 per cent reduction in the historical emissions from deforestation during 2014–2016.  

### 10. These results stem from the gross emission reductions based on the activity “avoided deforestation”. Of these emission reductions, 11.35 per cent (18,731,707 tCO2eq) is being offered to GCF in the funding proposal. During the period 2014–2016 and concerning the results achieved, Argentina has not requested any other payment from any other third party or financial entity or participated in voluntary carbon market schemes.  

### 11. The results achieved by Argentina are in part the outcome of the early development of REDD-plus readiness interventions in the country, which promoted a favourable framework for the approval and implementation of the ambitious PANByCC. The total volume of emission reductions achieved by Argentina in this period, including those proposed in the REDD-plus RBP pilot programme, are included in the technical annex to the BUR submitted to the UNFCCC secretariat. These results have undergone technical assessment in accordance with the decisions of the Conference of the Parties to the UNFCCC on REDD-plus.  

### 12. The achievement of the results contributed directly to GCF objectives and targets in the forest and land-use results area and furthermore, through the use of proceeds, will lead to a continued reduction in emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, and enhancement of forest stocks and conservation.  

### 13. Although the forest reference emission level is subnational (four forest regions), Argentina demonstrates ambition to scale up to the national level, mainly through the implementation of PANByCC and the link to the commitments in the LULUCF sector under Argentina’s NDC. The four regions considered at the subnational level are Parque Chaqueño, Selva Tucumano Boliviana (‘Yungas’), Selva Misionera (‘Selva Paranaense’) and Espinal (Caldenal and Nandubay districts), covering the centre zone of the country. The native forests in these four regions cover around 50 million hectares (ha) comprising 65 per cent of the area of

# GCF/B.27/02/Add.02  
## Page 146  

In Argentina, approximately 16 per cent of national greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions are related to deforestation, which had an increasing trend until 2008, when the Forest Law was enacted. Since then, deforestation has started to reduce in a varied manner among the different regions holding native forests. The above-mentioned four regions have heterogeneous socioeconomic and demographic features and dynamics; the drivers underlying deforestation and forest degradation are varied and respond to complex development processes.  

The average deforestation area in the period 2002–2018 amounted to 312,583 ha/year, decreasing from 394,374 ha in 2002 to 183,368 ha in 2018. Argentina lost 5,313,919 ha native forests in this period.  

The country continues to build an ambitious and powerful approach to REDD-plus based on the activities considered in PANByCC, which constitutes the national REDD-plus strategy, and the NDCs. The development of PANByCC as a public policy was supported by different partners, including the UN-REDD National Programme and the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility Readiness Fund.  

PANByCC builds upon five strategic structural pillars: governance; strengthening local communities; management, control and monitoring; native forest valorization; and knowledge management. These structural pillars serve as cornerstones for the prioritization of the activities to be implemented using GCF proceeds. In this sense, the use of proceeds offers the opportunity to support Argentina in addressing the different drivers of deforestation (mainly unsustainable livestock management and forest fires) and achieving additional REDD-plus results in the future.  

## 2.2 Paradigm shift potential                Scale: High  

The initiation of PANByCC helped Argentina to streamline and coordinate REDD-plus efforts through an ambitious and strategic approach. This has helped and is expected to continue to support the country in reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, and in the enhancement and conservation of forest stocks. It also supports the country in recording and reporting results in full compliance with UNFCCC provisions for REDD-plus results-based payments and GCF.  

The project will support the transformation of forest governance and forest landscape management to be undertaken by Argentina and allow for financial complementarity and coherence with other initiatives.  

## 15. The reinvestment of the GCF proceeds into PANByCC through supporting an ambitious set of interventions proposed and described in the funding proposal will help Argentina to further reduce emissions from deforestation in the coming years and continue to strengthen linkages with the private sector and indigenous peoples, which will in turn help the country to meet its national REDD-plus targets and commitments under its NDC.  

The investments from GCF seek to complement existing funding sources (e.g. World Bank, International Bank for Reconstruction and Development) and have the potential to attract other donors and financial mechanisms to blend in for the implementation of PANByCC.  

## 16. PANByCC establishes the ambitious actions required to reduce deforestation, forest degradation and associated GHG emissions through an integrated view of the forests and their role in the country’s development via the following strategic operational pillars:  

(1) sustainable forest management;  
(2) conservation in productive landscapes;  
(3) prevention of forest fires;  
(4) forest restoration and recovery; and  
(5) land-use planning.  

PANByCC is consistent with the general procedures and objectives of Argentina’s Law on minimum environmental protection standards for the enrichment, restoration, conservation, sustainable use and management of native forests (Forest Law). The GCF proceeds will support the implementation of all PANByCC strategic operational pillars.

# GCF/B.27/02/Add.02  
## Page 147  

### 17. Uncontrolled forest fires in Argentina, which are mainly human-caused, threaten the sustainability and health of the native forests. However, limited knowledge is available about the behaviour of such fires and their impacts on Argentinian forests’ composition, dynamics, functionality and resilience to potential impacts exacerbated by climate change. This project acknowledges the current and potential impacts of forest fires and uses a holistic approach to preventing and controlling fire occurrence while fostering the restoration of degraded forests through an enhanced response to forest fires (component 3). If proved successful, this set of approaches has the potential to be upscaled at the national level and even to other tropical and subtropical Andean countries with similar ecological and climatic conditions.  

### 18. Argentina has a solid legal and institutional framework for the sustainable management, conservation and restoration of forests; however, the prevailing causes of deforestation and forest degradation in the country, and particularly in the regions holding native forests, are related to the expansion of the use of forest lands for livestock-raising and agricultural practices, including key medium and large private sector players of the competitive agribusiness supply chains and business sector. To overcome the challenges linked with this land-use conundrum, the GCF proceeds are intended to be partially used to lay the foundation not only to promote sustainable models for forest and livestock management but also to attract private investment in developing and promoting commodity supply chains free from deforestation risks (e.g. timber, beef, soy and other agri-based industries).   

### 19. Although the project proposes an integral approach for the native forest ecosystems, timber from exotic species, plywood and pulp and paper industry from forest plantations are not included. However, the plantations subsector could offer innovative means for catalysing private sector investment, which could eventually contribute to the overall strategy for the restoration of degraded lands. It is recommended that an integral approach of the sector be maintained to ensure a coherent pathway in pursuing paradigm shift.  

### 20. Finally, a benefit-sharing mechanism proposed for the use of the proceeds has been built in accordance with the Forest Law and under a well-structured stakeholder consultation process; it will allow further implementation of PANByCC by defining and implementing mechanisms aligned to investments at the national and provincial level and targeting different beneficiaries, including forest producers and forest holders, indigenous peoples and local communities, small- and medium-scale cattle farmers, governmental entities and local enforcement authorities. The benefit-sharing scheme has been built and socialized under the challenging conditions brought about by the coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic, which meant that stakeholder consultation processes had to be undertaken remotely.  

### 2.3 Sustainable development potential             Scale: High  

### 21. The project will create an enabling environment for sustainable development by fostering systemic change at the local, provincial and national level. PANByCC counts among the country’s actions to foster sustainable development across the different strategic pillars while contributing to the Sustainable Development Goals at the national level and to the generation of social, economic and environmental co-benefits that seek to improve the livelihoods of the rural areas in Argentina.  

### 22. In addition, the proposed project is expected to engage and improve the livelihoods of women, who are mostly in disadvantaged positions. The project will strengthen governance in indigenous territories and provide support for improving their livelihoods.   

### 23. Argentina is a signatory to the International Labour Organization Convention on Indigenous and Tribal Peoples, among other instruments concerning indigenous peoples. The project is in compliance with provisions regarding environmental and social safeguards, emphasizing indigenous and gender dimensions. Indeed, the emphasis given to strengthening

indigenous peoples rights and incorporating their worldview into the development of the integral community plans (Output A.2) will result in social benefits related to cultural appropriation and respect for local practices and traditional knowledge, as well as to the management of the collective territories in accordance with the regulations in place. These local communities are characterized by having low or no capitalization capacity and unmet basic needs, with their time mostly dedicated to subsistence activities. Through the activities implemented under Output A.2 it is expected that productive activities (e.g. extensive animal breeding, sale of forest products surplus in local markets) can incorporate traditional practices of the territories such as hunting, gathering, fishing and crafting.  

24. A gender assessment and a gender action plan are key elements of the project. The project will build on this gender action plan aligned to PANByCC. It will use the various regulations of the policy instruments in the activities in full compliance with national policies, international treaties and the gender policies and guidelines of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO).  

25. The project addresses the participation of women in actions through specific indicators that will be included in the safeguards information system to monitor gender aspects and the generation of jobs from the implemented activities. The activities of the project will pay particular attention to barriers faced not only by women, but also by indigenous peoples, local communities and producers, which prevent them from being active players along the different agricultural and forestry supply chains that are expected to be strengthened by the project.  

26. The project targets poverty reduction and, through the focus on enhancing the provision of ecosystem services (e.g. biodiversity, regulation of the water cycle, control of erosion), will lead to increased resilience of the ecosystems and fewer expected impacts on the environment, resulting in improved conditions for the rural population. In addition, by fostering sustainable forest management, forest restoration and governance strengthening, favourable socioeconomic impacts are expected with project implementation.  

27. The project proposes to mainstream considerations for the support of a potential post-COVID-19 recovery phase in its actions. This may imply that in the selection process of plans and projects to be implemented under components 1, 2 and 3 an enhanced set of selection criteria could be tailored to give priority to those regions or provinces that experienced a higher socioeconomic impact from the COVID-19 pandemic. This approach could also apply for those livelihoods in local economies that have been damaged by the pandemic.  

## 2.4 Needs of the recipient                  Scale: High  

28. Argentina is a middle-income economy with a long history of macroeconomic instability and with limited national and subnational resources for the forest and land use sector. Argentina’s limited long-term economic performance has affected the country’s capacity to reduce poverty and fight inequality.  

29. The enactment of the Forest Law provided a framework for controlling deforestation, promoting land zoning and channelling public financial resources to the provinces to continue encouraging the sustainable use and management of the forest landscapes in the different regions holding native forests. The Forest Law allowed the creation and operationalization of the National Fund for Enrichment and Conservation of Native Forests (FNECBN), which has channelled around USD 287 million in a 10-year period (2010–2018) to the different provinces. However, the Argentinian Government has been unable to transfer the full amount to the provinces as foreseen in the Forest Law. ### Financial needs for implementation of the national REDD-plus strategy  
Argentina has received early financial support from different international entities and initiatives to implement REDD-plus actions in the country. While the exact amount of this support cannot be estimated, the funding proposal provides some indication of this financial support (around USD 28 million in grants and loans in the last five years). These collaborative efforts have supported Argentina in advancing on the implementation of the Warsaw Framework for REDD-plus and on engineering the building blocks for this REDD-plus RBP project. It should be noted that PANByCC does not contain a specific budget or investment plan.

### Action lines of PANByCC targeted by this project  
31. The action lines of PANByCC targeted by this project all have a strong focus on capacity-building of the various stakeholders at the national and provincial level (indigenous peoples, local communities, producers and government at the subnational and national level). There is a strong focus on strengthening the operational management of PANByCC. This will not only support the implementation of this project but also provide a strong platform for the entire strategy and indirectly support the implementation of all other action lines and activities that are part of such a strategy.

### Main beneficiaries  
32. The main beneficiaries of these payments include the rural population and indigenous peoples living in the four forest regions in Argentina. Indeed, indigenous peoples living in collective lands are among the most vulnerable groups in the country. In Argentina, around 955,032 individuals, or 2.38 per cent of the national population, are self-identified as descendants of or belonging to indigenous peoples. In addition, beneficiaries include small and medium-sized landholders, forest producers, small- and medium-scale cattle farmers and forest holders, as well as other key actors to dynamize the timber and non-timber forest products and services value chains (e.g. micro and small sawmills and carpentries and service providers).

### Country ownership  
2.5 Country ownership                    Scale: High  

33. The project is fully aligned with national policies and NDC priorities, and the proceeds will be used to reinvest in the full implementation of PANByCC. In its revised NDC, Argentina has committed to limit increases in emissions to 483 MtCO2eq by 2030. If implemented effectively, PANByCC can contribute to generating 27 MtCO2eq in emission reductions by 2030. The NDC is not intended to be perceived or understood as a stand-alone policy. In this pursuit, Argentina ratified the Law on Minimum Standards for Global Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation, which allowed a structure to be set up to mainstream climate change in Argentina’s long-term policies for development and in different sectors.

### Project preparation  
34. During project preparation, the national designated authority (NDA) has experienced a high level of engagement and country ownership. The NDA and the REDD-plus focal point have led the design and development of the project by providing feedback at several stages of the process. In particular, the NDA has been involved in the development of the institutional arrangements for the programme execution and has provided technical recommendations through a governmental technical advisory panel, which reviewed the funding proposal as per its internal procedures. Following its review of the proposal, the technical advisory panel recommended that the NDA issue a no-objection letter.

### Consultative process  
35. In the context of the preparation of this project, the Government of Argentina undertook a highly consultative process with relevant stakeholders, including the Federal Environmental Council and the National Climate Change Cabinet. Moreover, a series of online meetings have been held with other government sectors such as the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries, the Ministry of Women, Gender and Diversity and provincial governments, as well as the technical-academic sector, civil society organizations and indigenous peoples. ## 2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness             Scale: High  

Argentina’s REDD-plus results for 2014−2016 presented to GCF and the suggested budget both clearly highlight that a fixed price of USD 5 per tCO2eq will be applied to the project, which is cost-effective and in accordance with the TOR of the pilot programme. Furthermore, the payment for these results is being reinvested into a transformation of the forest sector, which will allow Argentina to continue its reduction of emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, and enhancement of forest carbon stocks.  

The reinvestment into PANByCC further helps Argentina to reach economies of scale together with other donors currently supporting PANByCC and brings different donors and partners together to improve complementarity and coherence. The GCF investment will complement ongoing support from the World Bank and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development.  

Numerous lessons learned and best practices are expected to arise from the implementation of activities and coordination with the other funding sources, which will be crucial not only for Argentina but also for other REDD-plus countries engaging in RBP. This will be important with regard to the private sector and in seeking to increase its engagement in REDD-plus finance, especially given the particular role of private sector actors in the timber, agricultural and livestock supply chains.   

Finally, Argentina has developed an interim accounting registry for REDD-plus results that allows the accounting of emissions traded and avoidance of double accounting of emission reductions. This system responds to the national monitoring, reporting and verification system and applies to all parties interested in registering GHG mitigation initiatives or projects, and claiming for payment for results or similar compensations, as well as those interested in registering initiatives that contribute to the national goals of emission reductions.

Implementation of Argentina’s Forest Law (Law No. 26.331; Law on Minimum Environmental Protection Standards for Native Forests) which was passed in 2007. The Forest Law provides a mandate for territorial planning for native forest by the provinces as well as incentives for their conservation and rehabilitation through the National Fund for Enrichment and Conservation of Native Forests. Under the Forest Law, owners of forest lands may implement management plans, conservation plans or land-use change plans (LUCP).  

43. The proceeds of RBP will be used to fund the implementation of Argentina’s PANByCC, which will have four components:   
(a) Territorial forest management, which involves planning and implementing forest management projects at the forest basin level, and designing and implementing integrated community plans;  
(b) Forest management with integrated livestock, which involves designing and implementing this approach in forests that are of medium conservation value (category II) and low conservation value (category III);    
(c) Forest restoration and enhanced response to forest fires, which involves implementing forest restoration projects and strategic plans for the prevention of forest fires and strengthening initial response capabilities in priority regions; and  
(d) Strengthening the technical capacities of national and provincial government institutions.

Informal settlers/agricultural colonists could be pressured to leave their land clearings without compensation or assistance. The AE’s due diligence report cited the alignment of national laws and regulations with its own ESS standards.

The ESA/screening checklist asserted that implementation of the Forest Law during the results period did not involve reforestation actions, land-use changes or use of any alien or non-native species. Regarding the use of proceeds, forest restoration and forest management with livestock integration may incentivize the expansion of economic activities into primary/native forests, affecting the quality of the forest and wildlife. These activities may also result in the introduction of non-native species which may turn out to be invasive and promote spread of new pests and diseases in the area. The project is addressing this risk through the exclusion list. In particular, activities that require significant conversion of critical natural habitats or legally protected areas or areas of high conservation value will not be considered, while the use of non-native species and genotypes to increase forest production would be restricted. In addition, forest restoration and forest management with livestock integration activities are allowed only in medium and low conservation value forests.

### 3.2 Gender policy  

59. The accredited entity (AE) has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan (GAP), and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of GCF.

60. The gender assessment provided by the AE demonstrates Argentina’s commitment to gender equality and women’s empowerment. Argentina has signed a number of international conventions that promote gender equality and the rights of women, including the Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination against Women incorporated into the National Constitution. In addition, Argentina ratified the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women (Belém do Pará Convention) and has a number of laws that promote women’s physical, economic and political autonomy. There are laws on gender quotas and gender parity, which seek to promote the participation of women in the various government branches on an equal footing with men. The creation of the Ministry of Women, Gender and Diversity was a milestone in the development of public policies with a gender perspective. Argentina adopted the 2030 Agenda for sustainable development.

# GCF/B.27/02/Add.02  
## Page 155  

Undertaking a commitment to mobilize the necessary resources to implement this gender approach, particularly taking into account the needs of people who are in a situation of greater vulnerability. Further, the Ministry of Women, Gender and Diversity launched the National Plan of Action against Gender-based Violence (2020–2022), which is a plan drawn up with the participation of over 3,000 people from across the country.

61. Although the enabling environment exists, there remain significant gender gaps and challenges to gender equality and women’s empowerment in Argentina. Regarding unpaid family and community-related care activities, women dedicate almost double the time to these compared with men. While there has been progress in reducing the gap in women’s employment (35 per cent in 2019), it still falls short of the employment rate of men, which is close to 50 per cent. Data also show that women have to have more education credentials than men to access paid work and women without a university degree have more difficulties than men in accessing paid work. In the lowest income group women outnumber men by 2 to 1 and the highest income decile men almost outnumbers women to the same ratio (1,029,050 men and 596,869 women). Progress in women’s representation in political positions has also been achieved but has not exceeded 25 per cent in government ministries. Similarly, the percentage of women who hold management positions in the private sector averages 31.6 per cent. The gap between men and women in land ownership in Latin America is historically very large, with women accounting for historically small percentages of landowners. Gender inequality in land ownership relates to male preference in inheritance, the privileges enjoyed by men in marriage, male bias in state land distribution (noting also paucity of sex disaggregated data on tenure) and in the land market, and this has exacerbated women’s unequal access to finance, training and capacity-building opportunities.

62. The advances of the agricultural frontier continue to threaten indigenous peoples’ ancestral territories, forests and fresh water sources. These water sources are often reservoirs of biodiversity, and vital spaces for indigenous communities for their daily lives, because the forest and water sources provide food (through the collection of fruits, medicinal herbs and honey, and hunting and fishing for animals) and energy resources (firewood). These effects can be even more significant for women and can be more challenging in situations of insecure land tenure. The challenges for women are further exacerbated by the fact that women are often not identified as agricultural producers. Women are often not considered valid stakeholders by those providing technical assistance, loans or training, thus re-enforcing women’s domestic and family-oriented roles as set by cultural norms. Women do work and make production-related decisions, although this is more within the realm of households. Farming-related matters are still identified as the responsibility of men, and technical assistance and loans continue to be targeted at men.

63. The assessment also indicates the differential impact of deforestation and forest degradation on women. Women’s roles and engagement with the forest are mostly not given attention because of the perceived low value of the roles women play in sustaining the well-being of their families. Women’s relationships with the forest and their surroundings are undermined and their use of forest is for household consumption and unpaid, whereas men’s engagement and work in forests is mostly remunerated and acknowledged as important economic activity. The assessment also indicates that the ongoing engagement and pressure of the real estate sector for the expansion of agriculture may have negative impacts on communities, depriving them of much-needed resources for sustaining their livelihoods, as well as resulting in loss of control over their territories and loss of local/community knowledge. The impacts of forest degradation and deforestation also give rise to specific vulnerabilities for women, such as restrictions in accessing resources and financing; low levels of participation in decision-making; and physical vulnerability due to situations of violence. Violence against women continues to be a prevalent issue, including in the forestry sector. Women continue to face numerous obstacles to gain access to forest governance spaces, including symbolic violence and exclusion by local leaders, traditional leaders and authorities.

# GCF/B.27/02/Add.02  
## Page 157  

### 69. Competing for carbon ownership  
Argentina’s legal system does not explicitly define the rights of ownership of emission reductions. However, MAyDS has a mandate for receiving, managing and administering payments for forest environmental services, and it has reached an agreement between the provinces in relation to GCF REDD-plus RBP. The agreement will exclude potential claims by the provinces that are the original owners of natural resources. In addition, the funding proposal confirms that there would be a covenant that no other party has a claim to results proposed to GCF.

### 70. The issue of tenure  
According to the funding proposal and the response of the AE, forests in Argentina are subject to various forms of tenure and it is estimated that 40 per cent of native forest cover is in areas without clearly defined tenure. The Local Enforcement Authority will approve management plans submitted by plan owners; the Forest Law allows plan owners to have different conditions of tenure, such as individual or collective ownership. The plan owners can be grouped into the same management plan. While this may enable indigenous and local communities without legal tenure to have access to the benefits of and participate in the sustainable forest management, the role of the Local Enforcement Authority will be critical to avoid any overlapping support to different groups over the same land or forest.

### 71. Disbursement plan  
The project implementation will be over six years. Accordingly, it is recommended that the disbursement of GCF financing be carried out over the six-year period corresponding to the implementation of the activities.

### 3.3.4. Compliance risk (medium risk)  
As per Article 9.03, paragraph (a), of the accreditation master agreement (AMA) between FAO and GCF, FAO will apply its own fiduciary principles and standards relating to any “know your customer” checks, anti-corruption, anti-money-laundering and countering the financing of terrorism, fraud, financial sanctions and embargoes to comply with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices. FAO has provided details as to how it will apply those fiduciary principles and standards in the implementation of this project. The proposed strategy and policies of the AE to be implemented are intended to serve as measures to mitigate and prevent money laundering, financing of terrorism and prohibited practices. Based on this additional information and the commitment of the AE to ensure that the activities to be undertaken are free from improper activities, compliance risk is assessed as medium.

### 3.3.5. Portfolio concentration risk (low risk)  
In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-material and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single proposal.

### 3.3.6. Conclusion  
It is recommended that any approval by the Board be made by considering the above-mentioned points.

# 3.4 Results monitoring and reporting

75. A monitoring and evaluation plan with detailed information on project activities and outputs, as well as the indicators and means of verification, will be carried out in accordance with the procedures of the AE and GCF and will include an initial report, annual performance reports to GCF, project implementation reviews, and a mid-term and a final evaluation.

76. Following the requirements of the TOR, the proposal will follow a simplified reporting procedure for which a specific template was developed by the Secretariat. The frequency of reporting will be annual following the AMA.

77. Monitoring the use of proceeds will be conducted at the main activity level in accordance with the description provided in the funding proposal.

# 3.5 Legal assessment

78. The AMA was signed with the AE on 8 June 2018 and became effective on 4 October 2018.

79. The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and has the capacity and authority to implement the project. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board (i) the AE will have obtained all its internal approvals and (ii) GCF will have received a certificate or legal opinion from the AE in form and substance satisfactory to GCF confirming that all final internal approvals by the AE have been obtained and that the entity has the authority and capacity to implement the project.

80. The proposed project will be implemented in the Argentine Republic, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, risks which need to be further assessed. The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with a background note on privileges and immunities to the Government of Argentina in April 2016. Discussions are currently ongoing, with the latest draft of the privileges and immunities agreement having been sent by the Secretariat to the Government of Argentina on 17 September 2019.

81. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both indicated that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF be made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

82. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

### Criteria Status (Yes/No) Remarks

#### Does the total volume of achieved results indicated in the proposal match the results indicated in the biennial update report (BUR) during the results period (31 December 2013 to 31 December 2018)?

**Yes**

The total volume of REDD-plus results achieved by Argentina and reported in the REDD-plus technical annex to the third BUR for the 2014–2016 period was 165,172,705.42 tCO2eq as the sum of 44,409,046 tCO2eq for 2014, 59,006,338 tCO2eq for 2015 and 61,757,321 tCO2eq for 2016.

#### Is the volume of achieved results offered to the pilot programme equal to or less than the total volume of achieved results indicated in the BUR during the results period?

**Yes**

Argentina is offering (from its 2014–2016 results) 18.73 MtCO2eq of net payable volume. This is less than the results achieved and reported in the BUR.

#### Is the expected volume of REDD-plus results to be achieved significant compared with the overall level of REDD-plus results achieved in the current funding proposal being submitted?

**Yes**

#### Is the total volume expected to be submitted to the pilot programme within the available allocation of funding for the pilot programme and below the cap per country?

**Yes**

Argentina is offering 18,73 MtCO2eq of net payable volume, in line with the indications included in the "modality and scope" of the GCF results-based payment pilot programme terms of reference.

These definitions were used by Argentina to report to the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations Forest Resources Assessment (FRA) 2015 and are an adaptation of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations definitions of forest land and other forest land to account for the national circumstances. ### ii) Is the FREL/FRL based on historical data and is it equal to or below the average annual historical emissions during the reference period, unless a country is a high forest cover/low deforestation (HFLD) country?  
The FREL is calculated on the basis of the annual average of CO2 emissions associated with “gross deforestation” using data from the historical reference period 2002–2013.  
Argentina did not include an adjustment.  

### iii) Is the FREL/FRL in accordance with the guidelines in United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) decision 12/CP.17?  
The technical assessment report (TAR) concludes that the information used by Argentina in constructing its FREL for the activity “reducing emissions from deforestation” is transparent and complete and in overall accordance with the guidelines for submission of information on reference levels. The AT considers that, in doing so, Argentina followed UNFCCC decision 1/CP.16, paragraph 71(b), on elaboration of a subnational FREL as an interim measure, and UNFCCC decision 12/CP.17, paragraph 10, on applying a stepwise approach.

## (vii) Is the FREL/FRL accurate?  
(The data and methodologies used neither overestimate nor underestimate emissions and/or removals during the reference period, so far as can be judged)  

The TAR identified several areas for future technical improvement that would improve the accuracy of the FREL, including:  

(a) Use of available species-specific wood densities for deriving the biomass values used to obtain the EFs to increase the accuracy of the estimates;  
(b) Establishment of additional sampling units in the forest regions of concern to enhance the accuracy of estimates;  
(c) Use of ground data to derive estimates of OTF instead of using a bibliographical review and expert judgment; and  
(d) Enhancement of the data acquisition process for the OTF class with new methods that allow for unequivocal identification of forest cover loss in this forest class.

## (viii) Have all REDD-plus activities that are significant sources of emissions been included?  

The subnational FREL proposed by Argentina includes CO2 emissions associated with “gross deforestation”. Gross deforestation is defined as the loss (clear-cut) of native forest cover from the conversion of forest land to other land-use categories.

The FREL includes the carbon pools above-ground biomass and below-ground biomass. For soil organic carbon, Argentina indicated that the country has a low coverage of organic soils and that these are not located in the regions covered by the FREL. The AT also assessed the potential significance of emissions from organic soils resulting from deforestation and concluded that they are probably not significant.

### In relation to the litter pool, Argentina justified the omission of this pool on the basis of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) default values, which assign to litter a contribution of 1.82 and 4.12 per cent of the above-ground biomass and below-ground biomass, respectively, depending on the forest region. The AT also notes that there was insufficient information in the modified submission to justify the exclusion of the deadwood pool in the construction of the FREL. The AT notes that, according to the information provided in the submission, Argentina intends to include emissions from dead organic matter and soil organic carbon pools in a future FREL submission, as part of the step-wise approach, on the basis of data collected during the second national native forest inventory.

### (x) Have all gases that are a significant source of emissions been included?  

The FREL includes only emissions of CO2. Non-CO2 emissions from fire are not included because the fire data available in the country are not robust enough to allow the estimation of emissions resulting from deforestation alone. The AT considers that the treatment of non-CO2 gases is an area for future technical improvement so as to maintain consistency with the GHG inventory included in the second BUR, noting that fire is one of the drivers of deforestation.

### (xi) Is the information provided in the construction of the FREL/FRL (data, methodologies and estimates) guided by the most recent applicable IPCC guidance and guidelines as adopted by the Conference of the Parties?  

For the construction of its FREL, Argentina used the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories (2006 IPCC Guidelines) as a basis for estimating the annual CO2 emissions from deforestation.

The TAR noted that Argentina included in its original FREL an assessment of the uncertainty associated with the EFs used to estimate CO2 emissions from deforestation. The Funding Proposal also details how the allocation of funds will be conducted: 72% allocated to investments at the territorial level in forest regions and provinces, and to strengthen the capacities of communities, indigenous peoples and smallholders. ## Page 179  

national forest inventory, the number of samples will be increased, as well as size, as part of the stepwise approach. In addition, in paragraph 22 of the TAR, the AT noted that for the other forest land class1 the country should use ground data instead of bibliographical review or expert judgment in order to improve the accuracy of the estimates. The AT also noted that the definition of other forest land adopted by the country might make it difficult to identify deforested areas through visual comparison of pairs of satellite images. Hence, the AT identified as an area for technical improvement the enhancement of the data acquisition process for the other forest land class with new methods so as to allow for unequivocal identification of forest cover loss in this forest class.

The score 1 is justified because issues were not resolved due to the limitation of time and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them;

### (b) B.1(viii) Have all REDD-plus activities that are significant sources of emissions been included:

The subnational FREL proposed by Argentina includes CO2 emissions associated only with “gross deforestation.”2 As reported in paragraph 14 of the TAR, Argentina provided information related to processes that lead to forest degradation (e.g. wood harvesting, grazing and agricultural activities, encroachment of exotic species into the native forest area) and acknowledged that, despite direct observations in the field suggesting that forest degradation is occurring, the quantification and spatial distribution of emissions from these processes prevented the inclusion of the activity “reducing emissions from forest degradation.” The AT noted that reducing emissions from forest degradation could also be significant. Argentina acknowledged in the modified FREL submission the relevance of this activity to the CO2 emissions and indicated efforts to address this issue in the future.

The score 1 is justified because issues were not resolved due to the limitation of data, and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them;

### (c) B.1(ix) Have all of the most significant pools been included:

Argentina includes above- and below-ground biomass in the construction of its FREL. Dead organic matter (i.e. deadwood and litter) and soil organic carbon in mineral and organic soils were not included. The justification for these omissions is the lack of reliable data for estimating dead organic matter and soil organic carbon in mineral soils, as well as the insignificance of emissions from litter when using default EFs provided by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), and the lack of organic soils in the territory covered by the FREL. As a result of the interaction with the AT, Argentina elaborated further on the exclusion of the carbon pools in the modified FREL submission, indicating that the country has a low coverage of organic soils and that these are not located in the regions covered by the FREL. Argentina emphasized that the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories do not provide default values that could be used as a basis for estimating changes in carbon stocks in deadwood, as was done for litter.

The AT noted that, according to the information provided in the submission, Argentina intends to include emissions from dead organic matter and soil organic carbon pools in a future FREL submission, as part of the stepwise approach, on the basis of data collected during the ongoing second national native forest inventory.

The score 1 is justified because issues were not resolved due to the limitation of time and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them;

1 Other forest land: land that forms a natural ecosystem with a tree cover of between 5 and 20 per cent native species, with trees that can reach a minimum height of 7 m; or with a tree cover equal to or greater than 20 per cent, where the trees present a height of less than 7 m; or that present at least 20 per cent of shrubland cover of native species, where the bushes have a minimum height of 0.5 m. It includes palms and reed bed formations.

2 Gross deforestation is defined as the loss (clear-cut) of native forest cover from the conversion of forest land to other land-use categories.

# GCF/B.27/02/Add.02  
## Page 180  

### (d) B.1(x) Have all gases that are a significant source of emissions been included:  
Argentina included only CO₂ emissions from gross deforestation in the FREL. The lack of robust data to allow the estimation of non-CO₂ emissions from fires associated with “reducing emissions from deforestation” prevented the inclusion of these gases.  

Paragraph 9 of the TAR notes that forest fires are among the main drivers of deforestation. However, the significance of fires to deforestation has not been assessed.  

The score 1 is justified because issues were not resolved due to the limitation of data and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them;  

### (e) B.1(xiv) How does the reference level for the results included in the proposal compare to the previous reference level that applies to the same area:  
The reference level used to calculate the emission reductions reported in the technical annex to the third biennial update report (BUR) is exactly the same in the modified FREL submission that has been technically assessed and covers exactly the same four regions of the country (subnational).  

The score 1 is justified because no adjustments were made or no previous FREL/FRL submission was made.  

### (f) B.2 (iv) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex consistent?  
(Were data and methodologies applied consistently over the results time series?):  

In paragraph 15 of the technical report on the technical analysis of the technical annex to the third BUR (TATR) (document FCCC/SBI/ICA/TATR.1/ARG), the LULUCF experts indicated that Argentina used consistent methodologies and data to generate activity data on gross deforestation, in particular assessing deforestation using the same approach, based on the same minimum mapping unit of 4–10 hectares (ha); used consistent methodologies and data to generate EFs, in particular the same above-and below-ground biomass pools, volumetric equations, biomass expansion factors, wood densities and carbon fractions; applied the same stratification of the four native forest regions for forest land; and included the same gases, namely CO₂ only.  

The TAP confirms that the results achieved are consistent with FREL. However, it was taken into consideration that some inconsistencies were identified by the LULUCF experts, in particular, the data provided by Argentina on the digital coverage of deforestation for 2014, 2015 and 2016; and the data from the official monitoring of deforestation reports are not always consistent with the estimates reported for the total deforested areas, particularly with regard to the deforested areas in the regions of Parque Chaqueño and Espinal for 2014 and 2015.  

The TAP and LULUCF experts also noted (TATR, para. 19) that the digital maps and monitoring reports present lower estimates of deforestation areas.  

The score 1 is justified because issues were not resolved due to the limitation of time and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them;  

### (g) B.2 (v) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex accurate?  
(Does the annex neither over- nor under-estimate emissions and/or removals?):  

In the TATR the LULUCF experts noted that Argentina applied the same EFs as those used in the construction of the FREL for each of the four forest regions considered and based on the carbon content in above- and below-ground biomass, by forest region and forest class.  

The issues raised during the technical assessment of the FREL were not resolved due to the limited time but will be improved when updated data and information derived from the national forest inventory will be completed, thus enhancing the accuracy of the estimates for REDD-plus reporting.  

The score 1 is justified because issues were not resolved due to the limitation of time and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them.

4. This funding proposal was submitted by Argentina under the GCF pilot programme for REDD-plus RBPs. Argentina offered to the programme 18.73 MtCO2eq emission reductions (ERs) achieved by the country for the period 2014–2016 from the reduction of gross deforestation. The total volume of REDD-plus results achieved by Argentina for this period is 165,172,705 tCO2eq reported in the REDD-plus technical annex to the third BUR that was assessed technically under the UNFCCC process in 2020. The assessment of the accuracy and reliability of the REDD-plus results is provided in the scorecard (annex to this report).  

5. These results are achieved by the country based on avoided deforestation against the FREL considering the following forest regions: Parque Chaqueño, Selva Misionera (Selva Paranaense), SelvaTucumano Boliviana (Yungas), and Espinal (only the Caldén and Ñandubay districts).   

6. Argentina’s FREL was calculated based on the historical average of gross emissions of the period 2002–2013. This period reflects the country’s deforestation dynamic since the years prior to the enactment of the Forest Law (2007) and the effects of its implementation. The four forest regions included in the FREL represent the majority of the Argentine territory covered by native forests (50 million hectares (ha)). These four regions also represent the area where the greatest historical forest cover loss occurs and therefore where most of national initiatives for the management and conservation of native forest are focused. About 66 per cent of the indigenous peoples of Argentina live in these four regions.  

7. An assessment of accuracy and reliability of Argentina’s FREL, which corresponds to annual average 101,141,848 tCO2eq for the period 2002–2013, is also provided in the scorecard (annex to this report) and is based on the technical assessment of Argentina’s FREL conducted under the UNFCCC technical assessment process in 2019. The TAR of the FREL, published on 25 November 2019, states that the information used by Argentina to construct the FREL is complete, transparent and in accordance with the guidelines included in the annex to UNFCCC decision 12/CP.17.  

8. In addition to the 18,731,707 tonnes of tCO2eq of net payable volume (which is 11 per cent of the total during the results period 2014–2016), Argentina offered to set aside/deactivate 2,997,073 tonnes of tCO2eq (16 per cent of the offered volume) from the remaining ERs achieved in the same period, as a buffer against the risk of reversal. During the assessment process the risk of reversal estimation was revised and the percentage of the volume to be set aside was increased to 40 per cent (7,492,683 tonnes of tCO2eq). The reversals risk analysis is based on a risk identification methodology related to non-permanence of ERs, developed by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility. The description of the methodology and the revised results are provided in annex 5 of the funding proposal.  

9. Under the Argentine UN-REDD National Programme, which started in April 2015 and finished in December 2019, the National Action Plan on Forests and Climate Change (known by its Spanish acronym, PANByCC) was developed by the country, and this is the country’s REDD-plus strategy. Argentina submitted the PANByCC to the UNFCCC process in January 2019. During the extensive process of consultation and participation to outline its national REDD-plus strategy, Argentina determined that only native forests would be considered within the REDD-plus framework.
